## Page 1


MIRROR SYMMETRY OF THE AFFINE TODA SYSTEMS
XIN JIN AND ZHIWEI YUN
Abstract. For a complex reductive group G, we prove a homological mirror symmetry between the
wrapped Fukaya category of the affine Toda system for G and coherent sheaves on the regular centralizer
group scheme for the Langlands dual group G∨.
This can be interpreted as a geometric Langlands
equivalence for P1 with mildest wild ramification at 0 and ∞.

1. Introduction
1.1. Toda system and its constructible quantization. The classical Toda system for a complex
reductive group G, as formulated by Kostant [26], is the group scheme of regular centralizers π : JG →
c := g // G. More precisely, the fiber of π over a point a ∈c is the centralizer CG(ea) where ea ∈g is any
regular element with image a in c.


## Page 3


MIRROR SYMMETRY OF THE AFFINE TODA SYSTEMS
3
Let U be a maximal unipotent subgroup of G and ψ : U →Ga is a homomorphism that is nontrivial
on each simple root subgroup. We call the “double coset”
(1.1)
(U, ψ)\G/(U, ψ)
the bi-Whittaker “space” of G. With the presence of ψ it is merely a symbol at this point, but the category
of sheaves on the bi-Whittaker space can be made sense of in various contexts. The relation between JG
and the bi-Whittaker space is clarified by the isomorphism
JG ∼= T ∗((U, ψ)\G/(U, ψ)) := (T ∗G)//(ψ,−ψ)U × U.
Here the right side means the Hamiltonian reduction of T ∗G under the left and right action of U by taking
the preimage of (dψ, −dψ) ∈n∗× n∗under the moment map and quotienting by U × U.
Therefore, a (constructible) quantization of JG should be the category of sheaves on the bi-Whittaker
space (1.1). In the D-module context, it means the derived category of D-modules on G with left and
right equivariant structures with respect to U against pullback of the exponential D-module along ψ. If
one works with ℓ-adic sheaves over schemes in characteristic p, the exponential D-module gets replaced
by the Artin-Schreier sheaf on Ga and the similar left and right (U, ψ)-equivariant derived category on G
makes sense. Alternatively, one can work with the wrapped Fukaya category W(JG) of JG as a model
for microsheaves, which gives yet another constructible quantization of JG. We call these constructible
quantizations of JG bi-Whittaker categories of G.
In all three contexts (D-modules, ℓ-adic and Fukaya), various authors have proved equivalences between
the constructible quantization of JG and coherent sheaves on spaces that are very close to T ∨// W, where
T ∨is the Langlands dual of a maximal torus T of G. In the D-module context, an analogous result
was proved by Ginzburg [18], Lonergan [29], Ben-Zvi and Gunningham [3] and Gannon [17]. In the ℓ-adic
setting, an analogous result was proved by Bezrukavnikov and Deshpande [5]. Most relevant to the current
paper is the following mirror description of the bi-Whittaker category in the Fukaya context, proved by
the first-named author. We state a simplified version here.
1.1.1. Theorem ([23, Theorem 7.7]). Assume G has connected center, then we have a canonical equivalence
W(JG) ≃Coh(T ∨// W)
sending the unit section of JG to the structure sheaf OT ∨//W .
1.2. Main constructions, results and conjectures. The purpose of this paper is two-fold: we study
the geometry and cohomology of the affine Toda system MG, and we prove a homological mirror symmetry
theorem for MG.
1.2.1. Affine Toda system. For any split reductive group G of rank r over a field k, we introduce a
symplectic algebraic variety MG of dimension 2r, the affine Toda system. The affine Toda system is
birationally equivalent to the periodic Toda system defined in [6] that has been studied extensively in the
mathematical physics literature. The space MG is constructed as the moduli space of G-Higgs bundles
over P1 with specific singularities at 0 and ∞. We show that it has the following features:
• MG is glued from (torsors of) Toda systems of G as well as its pseudo-Levi subgroups.
• There is a proper map fG : MG →AG, the Hitchin map, to an affine space AG that realizes MG
as a completely integrable system. General fibers of fG are finite unions of abelian varieties of
dimension r. This integrable system is birationally equivalent to the periodic Toda system in type
A, whose solutions are expressed in terms of theta functions on the Jacobians of spectral curves,
which are the fibers of the Hitchin map.
• MG admits another affine map MG →P to a weighted projective space P of dimension r that is
also a completely integrable system. This map records the isomorphism types of the underlying
G-bundles (with level structure) of the Higgs bundles.
• Combining the two integrable systems gives a finite flat map ν : MG →AG × P. It has degree
|W| on each connected component of G.

1.4. Geometric features of the homological mirror symmetry. We comment on some of the geo-
metric features of our HMS results and some connections to the literature.
1.4.1. HMS of multiplicative Coulomb branches. When G is semisimple, the B side of the mirror symmetry
JG∨,ad
G∨
is a (twisted) multiplicative Coulomb branch (MCB). As in Conjecture 1.2.5, M◦
G is expected to
be a hyperK¨ahler rotation of JG
Gsc. In particular, by viewing the real part of the holomorphic symplectic
form on M◦
G as the K¨ahler form on JG
Gsc, we can state our main result as HMS between MCB as follows.
1.4.2. Theorem. Assume Conjecture 1.2.5 (i) holds. For G semisimple, there is an equivalence of cate-
gories
W(JG
Gsc) ≃Coh(JGsc,∨
G∨
).
We briefly mention some of the recent developments on HMS of MCB.
First, in the abelian case, by the work of Gammage, McBreen and Webster [32, 14], HMS of multi-
plicative hypertoric varieties is established. Their approach is based on the calculation of the Lagrangian
skeleton and the category of microlocal sheaves on it.
Second, in the work of Aganagic, Danilenko, Li, Shende and Zhou [1], HMS of MCB from quiver gauge
theory, equipped with a superpotential, is established. They construct an embedding of a certain cylindrical
KLRW category into the corresponding Fukaya-Seidel category, and the B-model is a resolved additive
Coulomb branch. Their approach is based on counting J-holomorphic disks in symmetric products of
surfaces.
When G is of type A with matter 0, there should be a close relation between their result
and ours. However, our approach is quite different (see §1.5 below), which essentially avoids counting
holomorphic disks.
1.4.3. SYZ mirror symmetry. Strominger–Yau–Zaslow (SYZ) fibrations and T-duality is one of the guiding
principles in the study of mirror symmetry. The Hitchin map f◦
G : M◦
G →AG is an example of a SYZ
fibration. We comment on how to understand our HMS results conceptually from SYZ mirror symmetry.
Assume G is adjoint, e.g. PGLn, there is a canonical Hitchin section SI to the Hitchin fibration that
generates the wrapped Fukaya category of M◦
G. The calculation of the mirror reduces to wrapping the
Hitchin section and the calculation of the endomorphism algebra End(SI). In general, it is a challenging
1One may wonder about a purely affine analog of the bi-Whittaker space, namely (I+, ψ)\G((t))/(I+, ψ). However, sheaves
on this space are not interesting enough: when G is simply-connected, all sheaves are supported on the double cosets of central
elements of G.

1.4.4. Lagrangian skeleta: Example of G = PGL2. Lastly, we explain how to get a Lagrangian skeleton of
M◦
G in the case of G = PGL2, where the geometry is quite explicit. In this case, M◦
G has real dimension
4, and the SYZ fibration has exactly two singular fibers, each with a nodal singularity. There are two ways
to construct a Lagrangian skeleton.
First, using the SYZ fibration, we can construct a Lagrangian skeleton from a smooth fiber with two
attaching Lagrangian thimbles. The skeleton is homeomorphic to D2 ∪c+ T 2 ∪c−D2, where c+ and c−are
two primitive 1-cycles on T 2 with c+ ∩c−= ±2.
We can also construct the skeleton from a “special” Weinstein structure (cf.
Remark 5.6.5).
The
skeleton is homeomorphic to RP2 ∪RP1 RP2, where RP1 is the equator in each RP2 = S2/(Z/2Z).
These two skeleta, though look quite different, are both homotopy equivalent to RP2 ∨S2. In general, it
is not easy to describe Lagrangian skeleta from the SYZ fibrations. On the other hand, the second way of
constructing skeleta generalizes, in which one can explicitly describe the (generalized) Weinstein handles
(cf. Theorem 5.6.3 and Remark 5.6.5).

assume G is almost simple and simply-connected. For each proper subset J of the set eI of affine simple
roots of the loop group LG, we have the pseudo-Levi subgroup LJ of G that is isomorphic to the reductive
quotient of the standard parahoric subgroup PJ of LG.

2. The space MG as a Higgs moduli space
In this section we introduce the main geometric player of the paper, the affine Toda system MG, and
study its algebro-geometric properties. The space MG is a partial compactification of the periodic Toda
system. We show that it carries two different completely integrable system structures.
2.1. Definition of MG.
2.1.1. The curve. Let k be a field. Consider the curve X = P1 over k, with affine coordinate t. Denote by
0 and ∞the k-points of P1 defined by t = 0 and t = ∞respectively. Let τ = t−1 be the affine coordinate
at ∞.
2.1.2. Group-theoretic data. Let G be a split connected reductive group over k. We assume that char(k)
is either zero, or larger than twice the Coxeter number of each simple factor of G.
Choose a split maximal torus T of G and a pair of opposite Borel subgroups B and B−containing
T. Let N and N −be the unipotent radicals of B and B−, whose Lie algebras we denote by n and n−
respectively.
Let G((t)) and G((τ)) = G((t−1)) be the loop groups of G at 0 and ∞, with arc groups denoted by G[[t]]
and G[[τ]] = G[[t−1]].
Let I0 ⊂G[[t]] be the Iwahori subgroup obtained as the preimage of B under the reduction mod t map
G[[t]] →G. Similarly, let I∞⊂G[[τ]] be the Iwahori subgroup obtained as the preimage of B−under the

From the description above, we see that K−1 is canonically isomorphic to the Serre dual of K0[1], i.e.,
K0 ∼= Hom(K−1, ωP1). The map [−, φ] : K−1 →Hom(K−1, ωP1), viewed as a pairing K−1 ⊗K−1 →ωP1, is
alternating. This implies RΓ(P1, K) carries a perfect symplectic pairing. In particular, the classical tangent
space H0(P1, K) of MG at m = (E, F+
0 , F+
∞, φ) carries a symplectic form, and H−1(P1, K) (infinitesimal
automorphisms of m)is dual to the obstruction group H1(P1, K).
If we show that H−1(P1, K) = 0, i.e., points in MG do not have nonzero infinitesimal automorphisms,
then the obstruction group will also vanish, hence MG is a smooth Deligne-Mumford stack with a canonical
symplectic structure. Since deg K0 −deg K−1 = 2r, we see that χ(P1, K[1]) = 2r. Thus dim MG = 2r.
It remains to show that for any geometric point m ∈MG, Aut(m) is trivial as an algebraic group
(which implies in particular the infinitesimal automorphisms of m are trivial, and that MG is an algebraic
space).
Consider the following Hitchin moduli stack HG without level structure: it classifies pairs (E, φ) where
E is a G-torsor over P1 and φ is a section of Ad∗(E) ⊗ω ⊗O(0 + ∞)⊗2 ∼= Ad∗(E) ⊗O(2). We have a
natural map
ν : MG →HG
that forgets the N and N −-reductions F+
0 and F+
∞. The map ν is representable. For a geometric point
m = (E, F+
0 , F+
∞, φ) ∈MG, ν(m) lies in the open substack H♡
G ⊂HG where the Higgs field is generically
regular semisimple: this is because any element on the right side of (2.3) is regular semisimple.
By
[34, Cor.4.11.3], Aut(ν(m)) is isomorphic to a subgroup scheme of T, hence diagonalizable. Since ν is
representable, Aut(m) ,→Aut(ν(m)), hence Aut(m) is also diagonalizable. On the other hand, Aut(m)
is isomorphic to a subgroup scheme of I+
0 by evaluating at 0, which implies that Aut(m) is unipotent.
Combining these facts we conclude that Aut(m) has to be the trivial group scheme. This completes the
proof.
□

2.5. Hitchin fibers. Classically, periodic Toda systems are solved using theta functions on the Jacobians
of spectral curves, which are fibers of the Hitchin map fG. We give a modern account of the spectral
curves associated with MG in type A and Hitchin fibers in general, following the paradigm of Ngˆo [34].

3. Mirror symmetry for MG: statement and motivation
In this section we formulate the main result of the paper, a homological mirror symmetry between MG
and the group-theoretic regular centralizer for the Langlands dual G∨. We explain how our result fits into
the ramified geometric Langlands program.
3.1. Group-theoretic regular centralizer. In this section we make general discussions about the reg-
ular centralizer group scheme for a connected reductive group H over C. At times we will use a choice of
a maximal torus T ⊂H, a pair of opposite Borel subgroups B, B−containing T with unipotent radical
N, N −.

3.1.5. Steinberg cross-section. Recall a Coxeter element in the Weyl group W is a product w = si1 · · · sir,
where si1, si2, · · · , sir is any ordering of the set of simple reflections in W.
Let w be a Coxeter element in W and ˙w ∈NH(T) be a lifting of w. Let
S ˙w = N ˙w ∩˙wN −
be the Steinberg cross-section defined in [37, 8.9]. Then S ˙w is an affine space of dimension r consisting
of regular elements of H. When H is semisimple, S ˙w meets every regular conjugacy classes finitely many
times.
When H is simply-connected, the map S ˙w →SH = H // H is an isomorphism.
For semisimple H, the map S ˙w →SH is a π1(H)-torsor.
For a reductive H, the Steinberg cross-section S ˙w lies in a single fiber of H →Hab. To cover all regular
conjugacy classes of H, we consider the map
(3.6)
µ : S ˙w × (ZH)◦→SH
given by multiplication followed by projection to SH. Since S ˙w × (ZH)◦maps isomorphically to SH1 for
H1 = Hsc × (ZH)◦, we conclude that µ is a ker(H1 →H)-torsor.
3.1.6. Group-theoretic regular centralizer: the case where Hder is simply-connected. For a connected re-
ductive group H over C, let Hreg ⊂H be the open subset of regular elements. Let IH
Hreg = {(x, y ∈

3.1.7. Lemma. If Hder is simply-connected, then IH

Hreg. The group scheme IH

This implies that the map a : H × Hreg →Hreg ×SH Hreg given by a(y, x) = (yxy−1, x) is smooth. Base
change a along the diagonal map ∆H : Hreg ,→Hreg ×SH Hreg we obtain the projection IH

We next show the commutativity of IH

The descent statement is proved by the same argument of [34, Lemme 2.1.1].

3.2. Statement of main result. The main result of this paper is the following.
3.2.1. Theorem. Let G be a reductive group. Let M◦
G be the neutral component of MG. Then for any
compatible system of Kostant sections (cf. Definition 8.11.3), which always exists, there is an equivalence
of dg categories
(3.7)
W(M◦
G) ∼= Coh(JG∨,ad
G∨
).
Here W(M◦
G) denotes the wrapped Fukaya category of M◦
G, to be made precise in Section 5.
Moreover, if G of adjoint type, then the equivalence (3.7) sends the unique Kostant section SI in TG to
the structure sheaf of JG∨,ad
G∨
.
See Proposition 8.12.1 below about the images of Kostant sections in M◦
G under the mirror equivalence
for general reductive G.

compatible system of Kostant sections, one for each pseudo-Levi subgroup LJ of G. It can be calculated

3.3. Motivation from geometric Langlands. As we mentioned in §4.5, the definition of MG was
motivated by automorphic representations. In fact the equivalence (3.7) can be thought of as an instance
of categorical geometric Langlands correspondence with wild ramifications. We will now make this more
precise by relating the both sides of (3.7) to certain automorphic and spectral categories respectively.

4. Point-counting, cohomology and P=W for MG
When the base field is a finite field Fq, we give an explicit formula for the number of Fq-points on MG
in §4.1 and relate that to the counting of automorphic forms with specific local conditions in §4.5.
Over any algebraically closed base field k, we compute the cohomology of M◦
G and express it in terms
of root-theoretic data in §4.2. The result shows strong similarity with point-counting on the group regular
centralizer JGad
G
, which was done by Lusztig. To explain the similarity, in §4.4, we speculate the relationship
between M◦
G and JGad
G
by formulating a conjectural non-abelian Hodge correspondence between them,
and a P=W conjecture between their cohomology groups.
This section is not used in the rest of the paper.

4.2.2. Digression: sheaves on toric varieties. We need the following general observation for toric varieties.
Let Y be a T-toric variety, with open dense T-orbit j : Y0 ,→Y . Let L be a tame rank one local system
on Y0, corresponding to a character χL : π1(Y0)tame →Q
×

of T).
4.2.3. Lemma. Let L be a tame rank one local system on Y0 as above. Then any irreducible local system
appearing as a subquotient of (Rij∗L)|Yα for some i must be tame, and the corresponding character χ :
π1(Yα)tame →Q
×
ℓsatisfies χ ◦ρα = χL.
In particular, if L is nontrivial, then for any T-orbit Yα and any i ∈Z, (Rij∗L)|Yα does not contain
the trivial local system as a subquotient.
Proof. It is standard to reduce to the case where L has finite monodromy. Also we may replace T by a
quotient so that Y0 ∼= T. Now χL : π1(T)tame →Q
×
ℓhas finite image. Let p0 : eT →T be an isogeny from
another torus such that p∗
0L is trivial, i.e., χ|π1( e
T )tame is trivial. Let eY be the eT-toric variety constructed
using the same fans as Y . We have a finite map p : eY →Y restricting to p0 over Y0 ∼= T. Let pα : eYα →Yα
be the restriction of p over Yα.
Let Γ0 = ker(p0). Note that χL factors through Γ0 ∼= π1(Y0)tame/π1(eY0)tame. Also, Γ0 acts on eY and
p is Γ0-invariant. Let ej : eT ,→eY be the open embedding. Now Γ0 acts on E := Rp∗Rej∗Qℓ∼= Rj∗p0∗Qℓ,
which contains Rj∗L as the eigen summand for character χL ∈Hom(Γ0, Q
×
ℓ). Restricting E to Yα we get

4.4. Non-abelian Hodge package for MG and P=W conjecture. As explained in §3.3, MG can
be viewed as a Dolbeault moduli space for G-bundles on P1 with specific ramifications at 0 and ∞, while
JG can be viewed as a Betti moduli space for G-local systems with the same ramification conditions.
Following the general paradigm of Simpson correspondence [35], we propose
4.4.1. Conjecture. Let G be semisimple over C and Gsc be its simply-connected cover. Then there exists a
hyperK¨ahler structure on M◦
G such that JG
Gsc as a complex manifold is obtained from M◦
G by hyperK¨ahler
rotation. In particular, there is a homeomorphism M◦
G ≃JG
Gsc as C∞-manifolds.
Motivated by the P=W conjecture [9] for the usual Hitchin moduli space and character variety, we
propose a P=W conjecture for MG.

G ≃JG
Gsc in

5. Fukaya category of MG
Let ω = ωM◦
G be the real part of the canonical holomorphic symplectic form ωalg on M◦
G.
Our
first goal in this section is to construct suitable real Liouville 1-forms ϑ on M◦
G so that (M◦
G, ϑ) is a
Liouville manifold (with dϑ = ωM◦
G). We show that all such constructed Liouville manifold structures are
canonically equivalent (up to a contractible space of deformations). This enables us to set the wrapped
Fukaya category W(M◦
G) of M◦
G in a canonical way. The construction of ϑ occupies §5.2-§5.5, and the
main tool is by “modifying” the canonical Liouville 1-forms on TLJ, J ⊂ft eI, by constructing (various
versions of) “admissible” vector fields Z♭on the moment polytope △of P. We further show that for a nice
subspace of ϑ, M◦
G admits a Weinstein manifold structure (cf. §5.6).

5.6.3. Theorem.
(i) For any δ-admissible Liouville 1-form ϑ on MG, (MG, ϑ) is a Liouville man-
ifold. For any U ∈CIϑ,δ, the corresponding Dr gives a Liouville domain (whose boundary has
corners) whose completion is MG.
(ii) If ϑ is strongly δ-admissible, then there exists an exhausting Morse-Bott function f on MG, with
respect to which, (MG, ϑ) is a generalized Weinstein manifold. For any U ∈CIσ
ϑ,δ, the generalized
Weinstein handles are given by (5.14) for J ∈Pft(eI), for any fixed ϵ > 0 sufficiently small, whose
cores are diffeomorphic to D2|J| × Z(LJ)0,cpt × DdJ,m.
Proof. This is a direct consequence of Lemma 5.6.2.
□
5.6.4. Remark. The space of δ-admissible Liouville 1-forms ϑ is nonempty and convex. By the construc-
tion of Dr, it is clear that one can choose a single Dr such that for every δ-admissible ϑ, it gives a Liouville
domain whose completion is MG. By Gray stability, for any δ-admissible family ϑt, 0 ⩽t ⩽1, the de-
formation is conjugate to a compactly supported deformation of Liouville manifolds. Thus, the Liouville
manifold structures (MG, ϑ) are all deformation equivalent and they form a contractible space.

6. Quasi-coherent sheaves on certain Weil restrictions
In this section we prove a general statement (Theorem 6.2.2) describing quasi-coherent sheaves on
certain schemes involving Weil restrictions. It will be applied to describe quasi-coherent sheaves on the
regular centralizer group scheme for a reductive group.
6.1. Admissibility. Let k be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. Let W be a finite group.
Let Repk(W) be the category of finite-dimensional k-representations of W.

isomorphic to the regular representation k[W]. In particular, k ⊂C is identified with the W-invariants in
C.

6.2. Statement of main result.
6.2.1. Setup. Let k be a field of characteristic 0. Let W be a finite group. Let Q be a finite type k-scheme,
and let S = Q // W. Consider the quotient map
π : Q →S.

Let X be another k-scheme of finite type with a W-action and let ξ : X →Q be a quasi-projective
W-equivariant morphism. We can form the Weil restriction
RX = RQ/S(X).

8. Proof of the Mirror Theorem 3.2.1
In this section, we give the proof of the Mirror Theorem 3.2.1. Using results from §5–§7, the remaining
step is to construct an equivalence between the canonical limit diagram for calculating IndW(M◦
G) (§5.8)
and the canonical limit diagram for QCoh(JG∨,ad
G∨
) in Corollary 7.3.7. As stated in the Mirror Theorem,
such an equivalence depends on a choice of a compatible system of Kostant sections (cf. Definition 8.11.3
below).
We begin with a review of HMS for Lie algebra regular centralizers in §8.1. Building on this, in §8.2–
§8.10, for a reductive group H, we use monadicity on wrapped Fukaya categories (for a finite central
quotient H →H♭with Z(H♭) connected) and the canonical πZ(H)-action on W(TH) to establish the
canonical πZ(H)-equivariant mirror equivalence ΦH §8.10. Then in §8.11, we establish the compatibility
of ΦH with respect to restriction to a Levi subgroup L ⊂H containing T, which is encoded in the diagram
(8.70). For M◦
G, we assemble these diagrams into the natural commutative diagram (8.74) of coCartesian
fibrations over Pft(eI)op. We also show the existence of a compatible system of Kostant sections σ in
Lemma 8.11.3. Lastly, for any σ, we get an equivalence between the respective canonical limit diagrams
for IndW(M◦
G) and QCoh(JG∨,ad
G∨
) over Pft(eI)op. Taking limit categories on both sides, we get a desired
mirror equivalence Φσ. This finishes the proof of the Mirror Theoerem in §8.12.
8.1. Mirror symmetry for Lie algebra regular centralizers (review of [23]). Let H be any reduc-
tive group with a maximal torus T, a complete set of simple roots IH and Weyl group W. To simplify
notations, we will use either W(T H) or W(TH) to denote the wrapped Fukaya category of the Liouville
sector T H (which is also a generalized Weinstein sector) defined in §B.4.1 and §B.5.7.
We recall the main result from [23] for any reductive group H. For any finite set S, let P(S) (resp.
P†(S)) denote the power set of S (resp. P(S) −{S}). Recall Pft(eI) = {J ⊂ft eI} ⊊P(eI).
8.1.1. Main results from [23]. We state the HMS results and parabolic induction patterns from [23], for
reductive groups with connected centers. We give details in later sections on establishing HMS for general
reductive groups and parabolic induction patterns based on this. Although the latter has been established
for JH in [23], it is slightly different from the current setting where TH is a torsor of JH (cf. Remark 8.10.3
for the relation between HMS for them), and this difference becomes more significant when we consider
gluing wrapped Fukaya categories of them for calculating W(M◦
G) (cf. Corollary 5.8.1).

8.6.4. Convention about opposite groups and algebras. In the following, for any group or algebra homo-
morphism φ : A →B, let φop : Aop →Bop be the opposite version of φ.
Most of the time, the groups and algebras A that we encounter are commutative, so there is the trivial
identification Aop = A. However, for the algebras A∅and AH♭that we will focus on, we will identify them
with their respective opposite versions by a nontrivial involution—this is determined by the canonical
involution on the fundamental groupoid π(T ♭)op ∼
→π(T ♭) (cf. §8.6.5).
Most of the time, the distinction between A∅(resp. AH♭) and A∅,op (resp. Aop

8.11.3. Definition. We say a section of the left fibration P →Pft(eI)op (up to isomorphisms) is a compatible
system of Kostant sections.
8.11.4. Lemma. The left fibration P →Pft(eI)op has a section. Therefore, there exists a compatible system
of Kostant sections.
Proof. The obstruction to the existence of a section lies in H1(△, πZ), which vanishes by Lemma C.1.1.
□
8.11.5. Corollary. The left fibration WP →Pft(eI)op has a section. Therefore, ϖ (8.73) admits a πZ-
equivariant section bσ : P →WP over Pft(eI)op.
Proof. Choose any section s of P →Pft(eI)op, the obstruction to lifting s to a section σ of WP →Pft(eI)op
lies in H2(△, C×), which is trivial. Any lifting σ induces a πZ-equivariant section bσ : P →WP over
Pft(eI)op.
□
8.11.6. Remark. Since H1(△, C×) is trivial, the lifting σ of s is unique up to isomorphisms. Thus a com-
patible system of Kostant sections is also equivalent to a section of WP →Pft(eI)op (up to isomorphisms).

Appendix A. Tropical manifolds and admissible coverings
In this section, we introduce some convenient constructions and terminologies to be used in §5.2–5.7
and Appendix B. The key notions are tropical manifolds and admissible coverings.
A.1. Tropical manifolds. Let M be a (connected) real smooth manifold with corners. Then the corner
structure determines a natural stratification of M by M ◦, connected components of codimension i corners
contained in ∂M for i ⩾1. Denote the stratification of M by {Sα}α∈A and call A the indexed set of M.
Define a partial order on A given by α < β if and only if Sα ⊃Sβ. For each α, let Mα be the star of Sα,
i.e. Mα = S
β<α Sβ. Clearly, α < β ⇔Mα
open
⊂Mβ. Let dα = dim Sα and codα = codimSα = d −dα. Let
A≩α := {β ∈A : β ≩α} and A>α := {β ∈A : β > α}. Similarly, we define A≨α and A<α.
A.1.1. Definition. We say M is a tropical manifold of dimension d if
(1) For each α, we have a fixed isomorphism of manifolds with corners
να : Mα

A.1.4. Conventions on manifolds with convex/concave corners. Let M be an m-dimensional manifold with
convex/concave corners. For simplicity, we will assume that M is a submanifold with corners in RN, for
some sufficiently large N, in the following sense. There exists a smooth submanifold f
M ⊃M in RN
without boundary such that M is locally defined in f
M by the intersection and/or union of loci defined
by finitely many inequalities Csub-an(RN) ∋fi ⩾0, 1 ⩽i ⩽k, where Csub-an means the set of subanalytic
functions, and the collection {fi = 0}1⩽i⩽k (which are assumed to be smooth) and f
M have transverse
intersections. The corner structure on M gives a Whitney stratification SM = {Sj}j on M (with M ◦
being the open stratum). This will always hold for any manifold with corners that we encounter later.
We say V is a smooth vector field on M, if it satisfies the following:
(1) V is the restriction to M of a smooth vector field on Nb(M) in RN;
(2) V is stratified, i.e. the restriction of V to each stratum of M is tangent to the stratum.
For any open subset U in a manifold M with corners, let ∂◦U = ∂U ∩M ◦. Assume U ⊂M admits a
Whitney stratification SU satisfying that (1) ∂◦U ∩Sj, for each j, is the union of the strata in a subset
S′
U ⊂SU; (2) the collection of strata in SU −S′
U consists of those nonempty Sj ∩U (possibly not connected;
note that this requires that U = U −∂◦U). Then for a smooth vector field V defined in a neighborhood of
∂◦U, we say V is convex (resp. concave) along ∂◦U if for any Sj, the pairing of Vx with N ∗
x(Sj ∩U)Sj −{0}
is strictly positive (resp. negative), for x ∈Sj ∩∂◦U. Here N ∗
x(Sj ∩U)Sj ⊂T ∗Sj stands for the conormal
cone to Sj ∩U as an open subset in Sj defined in [25, Definition 5.3.6].
Recall that a map f : M →N between stratified manifolds (M, SM) and (N, TN) is said to be stratified
if (1) for every stratum Ti ∈TN, f −1(Ti) is a union of strata in SM; (2) if a stratum Sj ∈SM is mapped
to Ti, then f|Sj : Sj →Ti is submersive. We say a map f : M →Q between manifolds with corners is a
trivial stratified fiber bundle, if f is a stratified map with respect to the stratification on M and Q (induced
by the respective corner structures), and there exists q ∈Q and a stratified preserving homeomorphism ϕ
making the following diagram commutative
f −1(q) × Q

A.2. Admissible open coverings. Let M be a tropical manifold of dimension d. For a subset X ⊂M,
let ∂◦X := (∂X ∩M ◦) ⊂M.

A small remark is that Lemma A.2.9 (2) does not follow directly from the definition of an admissible
covering since the hypersurfaces in (2) need not all be transverse. It is natural to add Lemma A.2.9 (2)
into the definition of an admissible covering, but we prefer to make the definition shorter.
A.2.10. Definition. For any α ∈A, fix a codα-tuple of positive integers mα. Let Zmα be the vector field
on Rcodα
⩾0
defined as above. We say an admissible covering {(Cα, Iα) : α ∈A} of M is good with respect to
Zmα, α ∈A, if it satisfies the conditions in Lemma A.2.9.
More generally, for an admissible covering {(Cα, Iα) : α ∈B}, B ⊂A, of V, we say it is good with
respect to Zmα, α ∈B, if it satisfies the same condition in Lemma A.2.9 (2) with A replaced by B and
the following replacement of (1)
(1’) for any α ∈B and xν ∈Iα, ∂◦Cα ⊂Rcodα
⩾0
is good with respect to Zmα and some admissible covering
of a neighborhood of ∂◦Cα that contains the induced admissible covering Ux
α as the subcollection indexed
by B≨α (cf. Remark A.2.4 (i)).
A direct consequence of Lemma A.2.9 and its proof is the following.
A.2.11. Corollary. For any compact tropical manifold M (with a choice of mα, α ∈A), the poset of all
(good) admissible coverings is a filtered poset, and there is a cofinal sequence.
Appendix B. Backgrounds on Weinstein sectors and wrapped Fukaya categories
For any Liouville manifold/sector M, let Z denote the Liouville vector field. Let ∂M be the finite
boundary of M and let M ◦= M −∂M. For any submanifold Y ⊂M with ∂Y ⊂∂M (i.e. Y ∩M◦is
properly embedded) and Y is transverse to ∂M, that is invariant under the Liouville flow near ∂∞M, we
call Y a cylindrical submanifold of M. For any cylindrical submanifold Y , let NbZ(Y ) denote a cylindrical
open neighborhood of Y .
B.1. Sectorial coverings and descent of wrapped Fukaya categories. In this section, we recall
the definition of a Liouville/Weinstein sectorial covering and the descent property of wrapped Fukaya
categories from [16, §12.1].
B.1.1. Definition. Let (M, ω = dϑ) be a Liouville (resp. Weinstein up to deformations) sector.
(i) A finite collection of cylindrical hypersurfaces H1, · · · , Hn ⊂M is called sectorial if (1) their
characteristic foliations are ω-orthogonal over their intersections; (2) there exists a function Ii :
NbZ(Hi) →R that is linear near infinity (with respect to Z) such that dIi|char. fol. of Hi ̸= 0 (equiv-
alently, the Hamiltonian vector field XIi of Ii satisfies that XIi ̸∈THi), and dIi|char. fol. of Hj =
0, ∀j ̸= i (equivalently, XIi ∈THj, ∀j ̸= i). Here “char. fol. of Hi” is the shorthand of character-
istic foliation of Hi.
(ii) Let Mi, i = 1, · · · , n, be a cover of M. If each Mi is a Liouville subsector and the cylindrical
hypersurfaces Hi := ∂Mi ∩M ◦, i = 1, · · · , n, together with ∂M, are sectorial, then we say Mi, i =
1, · · · , n, form a Liouville sectorial covering.
B.1.2. Remark. For any sectorial collection of cylindrical hypersurfaces H1, · · · , Hn ⊂M, every finite
intersection among them is transverse, and T
j∈J⊂{1,··· ,n} Hj is coisotropic. The latter has a well defined

12.8 and 12.10]).

B.1.3. Definition. We say a sectorial covering is Weinstein if the convexification of each of the symplectic
reduction above is Weinstein (up to deformations).
B.1.4. Example. Let Q be a smooth compact manifold-with-boundary. Let Q1, · · · , Qn ⊂Q be codi-
mension 0 submanifolds-with-boundary such that S

∂Qn ∩Q◦, ∂Q are mutually transverse, then T ∗Q1, · · · , T ∗Qn is a Weinstein sectorial covering of T ∗Q.
B.1.5. Theorem (Theorem 1.35 [16]). For any Weinstein sectorial covering M1, · · · , Mn of a Liouville

B.5.5. A digression on posets. For any poset (P, <), let (C(P), ≺) be the poset of (non-empty) finite
strictly decreasing chains C = (c1 ≩· · · ≩ck) in P with C1 ≺C2 if and only if C1 is contained in C2 (this
is also known as the “barycentric subdivision”). Consider the natural functor
pP : C(P)op →P,
C = (c1 ≩· · · ≩ck) 7→ck.
B.5.6. Lemma.
(i) The functor pP : C(P)op →P is a Cartesian fibration with weakly contractible
fibers.
(ii) The functor pP : C(P)op →P is cofinal.
Proof. (i) For any arrow c < c′ in P and any chain C = (c1 ≩· · · ≩ck = c′) over c′, the Cartesian arrow
over c < c′ and ending at C is C′ := (c1 ≩· · · ≩ck = c′ ≩c) ≻C if c ≨c′ and is idC if c = c′. Since each
fiber contains a final object, the fibers are weakly contractible.
(ii) By [30, Theorem 4.1.3.1], it is equivalent to show that C(P)op ×P Pc/ is weakly contractible, for any
fixed c ∈P. Consider the natural inclusion C(P)op
end=c := {(c1 ≩· · · ≩ck) : ck = c} ⊂C(P)op ×P Pc/ =
{(c1 ≩· · · ≩ck) : ck > c}. First, this inclusion admits a right adjoint by adding c at the end to any chain
(c1 ≩· · · ≩ck), if ck ≩c and doing nothing if ck = c. Second, C(P)op
end=c admits a final object, hence it is
weakly contractible. Therefore, C(P)op ×P Pc/ is weakly contractible as well.
□

B.6. Constant integer gradings on holomorphic Lagrangians. For any complex symplectic vector
space (V 2n, ωC), let H(V, ωC) be the space of quaternionic structures (I = IV , J, K) on V that is compatible
with ωC in either of the following two equivalent senses:
(i) let g(v1, v2) = ReωC(v1, Jv2), then (I = IV , J, K; g) is a linear hyperkahler structure on V with
hyperkahler metric g, satisfying ωC = g(−, J−) + ig(−, K−);
(ii) ReωC(Jv1, Jv2) = ReωC(v1, v2) and ImωC(Jv1, Jv2) = −ImωC(v1, v2), and g = ReωC(−, J−) is a
metric.
It is easy to check using ωC(IV −, −) = iωC(−, −) = ωC(−, IV −) that (ii) implies that K is compatible
with ImωC and g(IV −, IV −) = ReωC(IV −, JIV −) = g(−, −).
B.6.1. Lemma. Given (V 2n, ωC), the space H(V, ωC) is diffeomorphic to Sp(2n, C)/Sp(n), where Sp(n) =
Sp(2n, C) ∩U(2n) = Sp(2n, C) ∩O(4n) is the compact form of Sp(2n, C).
In particular, H(V, ωC) is
contractible.
Proof. The proof is similar to the proof that the space of compatible linear complex structures J (E, ω) of
a given real symplectic vector space (E2n, ω) is diffeomorphic to Sp(2n, R)/U(n) (cf.[33, §2.5]). We omit
the details.
□
For a holomorphic symplectic manifold (M, ωC), we can talk about compatible (almost) quaternionic
structures (I = IM, J, K), where IM is the present complex structure on M, J, K are almost complex
structures, and for each x ∈M, (I = IM, J, K)x ∈H(TxM, (ωC)x).
Denote the space of compatible
(almost) quaternionic structures by H(M, ωC).

C.4. The tensor action of local systems on W(TH♭).


## Page 117


MIRROR SYMMETRY OF THE AFFINE TODA SYSTEMS

C.4.1. Geometric composition of Lagrangian correspondences of stopped Liouville manifolds. We will con-
sider Lagrangian correspondences of stopped Liouville manifolds coming from exact symplectic morphisms
that preserve the stops (and their transpose). Such a Lagrangian correspondence L ⊂(X, fX)−× (Y, fY )
will hit the stop of the product at ∞. It is standard to use a small generic negative Hamiltonian pertur-
bation of L to avoid the stop, which however does unpleasant things on the geometric composition.
We comment on how to easily avoid such an issue. One can replace (X, fX) (resp. (Y, fY )) by its
stabilization ( b
X, f)s = ( b
X, f) × (Cz, {±∞}) (resp. (Y, fY )s) and one replaces L by L × ∆(Cz,{±∞}). Then
one just needs to perturb ∆(Cz,{±∞}) by a small generic negative Hamiltonian. This can be easily realized
by replacing ∆(Cz,{±∞}) by the graph Γ−θ of a small clockwise rotation of Cz by 0 < θ ≪π
2 . Then the
geometric composition of two Lagrangian correspondences L1 and L2 is realized by (L1 ◦L2) × Γ−2θ ∼=
(L1◦L2)×Γ−θ, where L1◦L2 is the geometric composition of L1◦L2 without perturbation and Γ−2θ ∼= Γ−θ
is the canonical isomorphism.
C.4.2. The tensor action of Loc(X)bd,⊗on W(X). Let Loc(X)bd,⊗be the symmetric monoidal category
of local systems on X with bounded cohomology stalks, i.e. the cohomology of each stalk should have
bounded degrees and should be perfect modules in each degree. For any Weinstein sector X, we have the
natural tensor action by Loc(X)bd,⊗on W(X). If we consider IndW(X) instead of W(X), then we have
the tensor action of Loc(X)⊗. If we identify W(X) with wrapped microlocal sheaves on the Lagrangian
skeleton of X, then the action by Loc(X)bd,⊗is given by tensoring a microlocal sheaf by a local system
L ∈Loc(X)bd,⊗.
On W(X), the action is given by the monoidal embedding
ι : Loc(X)bd,⊗,→W∆X(X−× X)
L 7→(∆X, b, L)
where (1) W∆X(X−×X) ⊂W(X−×X) is the monoidal full subcategory generated by ∆X; (2) (∆X, b, L)
is the object that equip ∆X with the standard brane structure and the local system L. Here to make
∆X a well defined object in IndW∆X(X−× X), we replace the sector X by its corresponding stopped
Liouville manifold ( b
X, f), and further its stabilization ( b
X, f)s as in §C.4.1. It is trivial to see in this case
that geometric compositions involving (∆X, b, L) agrees with algebraic compositions.
Alternatively, one can define the action of Loc(X)bd,♡;⊗on W(X) by explicit description of the action
on objects, morphisms and A∞-compositions, and this is sufficient for our purpose below.
Let L ∈
Loc(X)bd,♡. On each object (L, b, E) where b represents a brane structure on L and E ∈Loc(L)bd,♡, the
action by L is just tensoring E by L|L. On the Floer cochain complex of two objects (Li, bi, Ei), i = 1, 2,
the action changes each component Hom(E1|p, E2|p)[−deg p] for p ∈L1 ∩L2 to Hom(E1|p ⊗L|p, E2|p ⊗
L|p)[−deg p]. For the differential on the Floer cochain complex, as well as any A∞-compositions involving
“counting holomorphic polygons”, the action is the obvious one by taking into account of parallel transport
of L along the edges of any polygon. Moreover, for any Hamiltonian isotopy ϕt, ϕ0 = id, t ∈[0, 1], on X, it
defines a canonical isomorphism (ϕ1)∗L ∼= L through parallel transport along the paths ϕt(p), p ∈X, t ∈
[0, 1] (and there are canonical isomorphisms between isomorphisms for homotopies between Hamiltonian
isotopies and so on, i.e. we have the canonical ∞-groupoid of Ham(X) acting on Loc(X)). Here Ham(X)
is in the same sense as in [15]. Then it is straightforward to see that this gives a well defined action of
Loc(X)bd,♡;⊗on W(X).

the canonical tensor action of Loc(TH♭)bd,⊗on W(TH♭) in §C.4.2, which is canonically compatible with
restriction and co-restriction functors.
We will denote the tensor action by

C.5. The canonical πZ(H)-action on W(TH). There are several equivalent models for describing the
canonical πZ(H)-action on W(TH). Since Z(H) is a K(π, 1)-space, it is direct to check that different
models match. On microlocal sheaves, the πZ(H)-action is given by the natural πZ(H)cpt-action on the
Lagrangian skeleton (here we have an easy situation that Z(H)cpt preserves the Lagrangian skeleton).
